People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, doctor. Thank you for seeing me. Um, well, to be completely honest, I'm here because my communication has just gotten... well, people are saying it's completely derailed. I'm an artist, and a big part of my job isn't just making the art, you know. I have my bachelor's degree in this, so I know how important the professional side is—writing artist statements, keeping really detailed client notes, pitching concepts in meetings with gallery owners or commissioners. It's a lot of talking and writing. 

But lately, I'll sit down to write a statement, or I'll be right in the middle of a meeting with a client, and my brain just sees all these incredible, intricate connections. So I'll start explaining the physical textures of a piece, like the literal materials and how they catch the light, but then that immediately connects in my head to the financial budgets for the project, and then from the budget numbers I start seeing these secret meanings that tie back into the artwork's core conceptual message. And to me, it's all one big, beautiful, interconnected web. It makes perfect sense. 

But everyone else... they just look at me like I'm speaking a completely foreign language. My clients, my peers, they keep stopping me and telling me they absolutely cannot follow what I’m trying to explain. They say I'm just jumping wildly from textures to budgets to these secret meanings without any bridge between them. It's incredibly frustrating because I feel like I'm being so perfectly clear, but my client notes are apparently a mess to anyone but me, my artist statements are getting flagged for being confusing, and I just can't seem to make people understand how it all fits together. It's really starting to impact my work, which is why I finally agreed to come in.
=====================================

Differential includes psychotic disorders, bipolar disorder with psychotic features, severe depression with psychotic features, and autism spectrum disorder (given artist background and detailed perceptual focus). Well, yes, actually. It's funny you ask that because it really has been a whole... a whole thing, honestly. For stretches of time, I just feel completely lit up, you know? Like this current of electricity is just buzzing right under my skin, and I feel almost unstoppable. I'll be in the studio, and every single idea I have feels so profoundly brilliant, like I'm finally tapping into the absolute truth of what art is supposed to be. I'll just go and go and go, and the energy is just so intense and expansive. 

But then... it's like a coin flip. I can be riding that high, feeling so connected to everything, and then I'll be talking to a collaborator or a gallery rep, and they'll make just one tiny comment. Maybe they ask a question about the timeline, or they don't immediately grasp the concept I'm explaining, and I just... I snap. And I mean really snap. The irritation just flares up so fast and hot, and I'll lash out in a way that I know, even as it's happening, feels so much bigger and more aggressive than the situation actually warrants. It's like my patience just evaporates instantly. So it's this weird mix of feeling like a glowing genius and then suddenly being so fiercely irritated that I can barely stand to be in the same room as people who aren't on my exact wavelength.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, that's a really interesting question, because when I try to map it all out in my head, it's not like... it's not like a light switch that just flips on and off, you know? It's more like... like a tide, maybe? But a really aggressive tide. If I look back over the whole year, or at least the past several months, these stretches where I'm just bursting with energy, where I'm producing art at this incredible rate and feeling so brilliant, but also getting so incredibly irritated with everyone—those periods have definitely taken up the majority of the time. 

It's actually been kind of exhausting to think about, because even though the intensity sort of rises and falls—like, some weeks I'm just buzzing at a level ten, and maybe the next week it dips to a six or a seven, where I'm still energized but maybe not snapping at people quite as violently—it's still there. The elevated state, that whole rushing current of ideas and irritation, it's been the dominant theme of my year. I'll have these brief windows where things feel a little calmer, maybe a bit more grounded, where I can actually sit and drink a cup of coffee without feeling like I need to simultaneously draft three artist statements and sketch a new sculpture. But those calmer stretches are definitely the exception, not the rule. The energized, irritable, overproductive stretches have just taken up way more of the year than the calm parts. It's like my baseline has just shifted upwards, and even when it dips, it never really goes all the way back down to what used to be normal for me.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thank you for seeing me. Um, well, to be completely honest, I'm here because my communication has just gotten... well, people are saying it's completely derailed. I'm an artist, and a big part of my job isn't just making the art, you know. I have my bachelor's degree in this, so I know how important the professional side is—writing artist statements, keeping really detailed client notes, pitching concepts in meetings with gallery owners or commissioners. It's a lot of talking and writing. \n\nBut lately, I'll sit down to write a statement, or I'll be right in the middle of a meeting with a client, and my brain just sees all these incredible, intricate connections. So I'll start explaining the physical textures of a piece, like the literal materials and how they catch the light, but then that immediately connects in my head to the financial budgets for the project, and then from the budget numbers I start seeing these secret meanings that tie back into the artwork's core conceptual message. And to me, it's all one big, beautiful, interconnected web. It makes perfect sense. \n\nBut everyone else... they just look at me like I'm speaking a completely foreign language. My clients, my peers, they keep stopping me and telling me they absolutely cannot follow what I’m trying to explain. They say I'm just jumping wildly from textures to budgets to these secret meanings without any bridge between them. It's incredibly frustrating because I feel like I'm being so perfectly clear, but my client notes are apparently a mess to anyone but me, my artist statements are getting flagged for being confusing, and I just can't seem to make people understand how it all fits together. It's really starting to impact my work, which is why I finally agreed to come in.\n\nDoctor: Over the same time period when you've noticed these communication difficulties, how has your mood been? Have you been feeling unusually energetic, more talkative than normal, or like you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. It's funny you ask that because it really has been a whole... a whole thing, honestly. For stretches of time, I just feel completely lit up, you know? Like this current of electricity is just buzzing right under my skin, and I feel almost unstoppable. I'll be in the studio, and every single idea I have feels so profoundly brilliant, like I'm finally tapping into the absolute truth of what art is supposed to be. I'll just go and go and go, and the energy is just so intense and expansive. \n\nBut then... it's like a coin flip. I can be riding that high, feeling so connected to everything, and then I'll be talking to a collaborator or a gallery rep, and they'll make just one tiny comment. Maybe they ask a question about the timeline, or they don't immediately grasp the concept I'm explaining, and I just... I snap. And I mean really snap. The irritation just flares up so fast and hot, and I'll lash out in a way that I know, even as it's happening, feels so much bigger and more aggressive than the situation actually warrants. It's like my patience just evaporates instantly. So it's this weird mix of feeling like a glowing genius and then suddenly being so fiercely irritated that I can barely stand to be in the same room as people who aren't on my exact wavelength.\n\nDoctor: When you think back over the past few months, have these periods of feeling energized and brilliant, followed by irritability, come in distinct episodes—like they have a clear start and finish? Or has it been more of a continuous state that's just been going on and on?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a really interesting question, because when I try to map it all out in my head, it's not like... it's not like a light switch that just flips on and off, you know? It's more like... like a tide, maybe? But a really aggressive tide. If I look back over the whole year, or at least the past several months, these stretches where I'm just bursting with energy, where I'm producing art at this incredible rate and feeling so brilliant, but also getting so incredibly irritated with everyone—those periods have definitely taken up the majority of the time. \n\nIt's actually been kind of exhausting to think about, because even though the intensity sort of rises and falls—like, some weeks I'm just buzzing at a level ten, and maybe the next week it dips to a six or a seven, where I'm still energized but maybe not snapping at people quite as violently—it's still there. The elevated state, that whole rushing current of ideas and irritation, it's been the dominant theme of my year. I'll have these brief windows where things feel a little calmer, maybe a bit more grounded, where I can actually sit and drink a cup of coffee without feeling like I need to simultaneously draft three artist statements and sketch a new sculpture. But those calmer stretches are definitely the exception, not the rule. The energized, irritable, overproductive stretches have just taken up way more of the year than the calm parts. It's like my baseline has just shifted upwards, and even when it dips, it never really goes all the way back down to what used to be normal for me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring those calmer stretches you mentioned—the exceptions rather than the rule—did you ever experience periods where your mood actually dipped low? Like feeling sad, hopeless, or depressed, rather than just less energized?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no... not really. I mean, when I think about those calmer stretches, the ones where the buzzing finally quiets down a bit and I can actually just, you know, sit still and drink a cup of coffee without feeling like I need to immediately start drafting three different artist statements and sketching out a new sculpture all at the exact same time... during those times, I wouldn't say I feel sad. Or hopeless. It's really just... an absence of that intense, electric current. 

It's actually kind of a relief, to be honest. It's like the volume on the world finally gets turned down from a screaming ten back to a manageable level. I don't feel depressed, I don't feel like crying or like everything is pointless—nothing like that. It's more just... neutral? Like I'm just existing in a normal, quiet space for a little while. I can actually focus on the mundane things, like organizing my studio space, which, let me tell you, gets completely chaotic when I'm in one of my highly energized phases. I'll have materials strewn everywhere—clay, wire, different grades of sandpaper, sketchbooks just left open on every available surface. So when the calm hits, I just spend time putting all of that back in order. But I don't feel down about it. I don't feel that heavy, sinking feeling that people talk about when they're depressed. It's just... quiet. A break from the relentless pushing and the snapping at people. Just a bit of peace before the tide comes rushing back in again. So, no, definitely not sad or hopeless. Just... resting, I guess you could say.

projects have been part of my life for about 10 months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S021\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Over the same time period when you've noticed these communication difficulties, how has your mood been? Have you been feeling unusually energetic, more talkative than normal, or like you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. It's funny you ask that because it really has been a whole... a whole thing, honestly. For stretches of time, I just feel completely lit up, you know? Like this current of electricity is just buzzing right under my skin, and I feel almost unstoppable. I'll be in the studio, and every single idea I have feels so profoundly brilliant, like I'm finally tapping into the absolute truth of what art is supposed to be. I'll just go and go and go, and the energy is just so intense and expansive. \n\nBut then... it's like a coin flip. I can be riding that high, feeling so connected to everything, and then I'll be talking to a collaborator or a gallery rep, and they'll make just one tiny comment. Maybe they ask a question about the timeline, or they don't immediately grasp the concept I'm explaining, and I just... I snap. And I mean really snap. The irritation just flares up so fast and hot, and I'll lash out in a way that I know, even as it's happening, feels so much bigger and more aggressive than the situation actually warrants. It's like my patience just evaporates instantly. So it's this weird mix of feeling like a glowing genius and then suddenly being so fiercely irritated that I can barely stand to be in the same room as people who aren't on my exact wavelength.\n\nDoctor: When you think back over the past few months, have these periods of feeling energized and brilliant, followed by irritability, come in distinct episodes—like they have a clear start and finish? Or has it been more of a continuous state that's just been going on and on?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a really interesting question, because when I try to map it all out in my head, it's not like... it's not like a light switch that just flips on and off, you know? It's more like... like a tide, maybe? But a really aggressive tide. If I look back over the whole year, or at least the past several months, these stretches where I'm just bursting with energy, where I'm producing art at this incredible rate and feeling so brilliant, but also getting so incredibly irritated with everyone—those periods have definitely taken up the majority of the time. \n\nIt's actually been kind of exhausting to think about, because even though the intensity sort of rises and falls—like, some weeks I'm just buzzing at a level ten, and maybe the next week it dips to a six or a seven, where I'm still energized but maybe not snapping at people quite as violently—it's still there. The elevated state, that whole rushing current of ideas and irritation, it's been the dominant theme of my year. I'll have these brief windows where things feel a little calmer, maybe a bit more grounded, where I can actually sit and drink a cup of coffee without feeling like I need to simultaneously draft three artist statements and sketch a new sculpture. But those calmer stretches are definitely the exception, not the rule. The energized, irritable, overproductive stretches have just taken up way more of the year than the calm parts. It's like my baseline has just shifted upwards, and even when it dips, it never really goes all the way back down to what used to be normal for me.\n\nDoctor: During those calmer stretches you mentioned—the exceptions rather than the rule—did you ever experience periods where your mood actually dipped low? Like feeling sad, hopeless, or depressed, rather than just less energized?\n\nPatient: Well, no... not really. I mean, when I think about those calmer stretches, the ones where the buzzing finally quiets down a bit and I can actually just, you know, sit still and drink a cup of coffee without feeling like I need to immediately start drafting three different artist statements and sketching out a new sculpture all at the exact same time... during those times, I wouldn't say I feel sad. Or hopeless. It's really just... an absence of that intense, electric current. \n\nIt's actually kind of a relief, to be honest. It's like the volume on the world finally gets turned down from a screaming ten back to a manageable level. I don't feel depressed, I don't feel like crying or like everything is pointless—nothing like that. It's more just... neutral? Like I'm just existing in a normal, quiet space for a little while. I can actually focus on the mundane things, like organizing my studio space, which, let me tell you, gets completely chaotic when I'm in one of my highly energized phases. I'll have materials strewn everywhere—clay, wire, different grades of sandpaper, sketchbooks just left open on every available surface. So when the calm hits, I just spend time putting all of that back in order. But I don't feel down about it. I don't feel that heavy, sinking feeling that people talk about when they're depressed. It's just... quiet. A break from the relentless pushing and the snapping at people. Just a bit of peace before the tide comes rushing back in again. So, no, definitely not sad or hopeless. Just... resting, I guess you could say.\n\n[Last Question]\nLooking back over the past year or so, have there been any distinct periods—maybe lasting days or weeks at a time—where you felt noticeably down or depressed, where things felt heavy or hopeless, and where you had less energy or interest in your art or other activities you normally enjoy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, like I was just saying, I really haven't had anything like that. It's funny, because people always assume that if you're an artist, you must have these deep, dark, brooding periods where you just stare at a blank canvas and cry or something, but that's just not me. I don't get those heavy, sinking, hopeless feelings at all. I mean, if I look back over the whole year—and it's been a really wild year, honestly, with all the gallery meetings and the new series I've been working on—I can't think of a single time where I felt depressed or lost interest in my work. 

My art is my entire life, you know? Even during those quiet stretches I mentioned, where I'm just organizing my studio and putting away the clay and the wire, I'm still interested in it. I'm still thinking about the next piece, even if I'm not actively, frantically building it right that second. I don't ever lose that drive or that passion. It just shifts gears. So, no, I've never had a period lasting days or weeks where I felt down or heavy or like I didn't want to create. It's always just been the high energy, the buzzing, the irritation, and then those brief, neutral resting periods where I just catch my breath. But never depression. Never anything close to that. It's almost like I don't even have the capacity for it, my brain is just wired to keep moving forward, even when it's moving a little too fast for everyone else around me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, if I really sit back and try to pinpoint exactly when this whole... this whole rushing river of energy started, it's actually been quite a while. I mean, it's not like I woke up one Tuesday and suddenly everything was dialed up to an eleven, but if I trace it back, I'd say it's been about ten months. 

Yeah, ten months of this. It was right around the time I was starting to conceptualize that large-scale mixed media installation I was telling you about—the one with the suspended wire and the terracotta fragments. I remember I was at the art supply store, and I had this sudden, absolute clarity about how the whole thing needed to come together, and I just started buying everything. And from that point on, it was just... go, go, go. The energized feeling, the irritable stretches where I just couldn't stand anyone interrupting my flow, the staying up until three or four in the morning because I had too many projects in my head and too little need for sleep... that's all been a constant companion for about ten months now. 

Before that, I guess things were more normal. I mean, I was still an artist, still working, but I wasn't producing at this frantic, almost electric pace. I would actually sleep a solid eight hours, which honestly sounds a little boring to me now, but back then it was just what I did. But for the last ten months, it's just been this continuous stretch of high energy, too many ideas, and snapping at anyone who gets in my way. Even with those little brief moments of quiet I mentioned earlier, the overarching theme, the main event, has been this elevated state for almost a year now. It just hasn't really stopped since that day at the supply store.

Or has it been more of a continuous state that's just been going on and on?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a really interesting question, because when I try to map it all out in my head, it's not like... it's not like a light switch that just flips on and off, you know? It's more like... like a tide, maybe? But a really aggressive tide. If I look back over the whole year, or at least the past several months, these stretches where I'm just bursting with energy, where I'm producing art at this incredible rate and feeling so brilliant, but also getting so incredibly irritated with everyone—those periods have definitely taken up the majority of the time. \n\nIt's actually been kind of exhausting to think about, because even though the intensity sort of rises and falls—like, some weeks I'm just buzzing at a level ten, and maybe the next week it dips to a six or a seven, where I'm still energized but maybe not snapping at people quite as violently—it's still there. The elevated state, that whole rushing current of ideas and irritation, it's been the dominant theme of my year. I'll have these brief windows where things feel a little calmer, maybe a bit more grounded, where I can actually sit and drink a cup of coffee without feeling like I need to simultaneously draft three artist statements and sketch a new sculpture. But those calmer stretches are definitely the exception, not the rule. The energized, irritable, overproductive stretches have just taken up way more of the year than the calm parts. It's like my baseline has just shifted upwards, and even when it dips, it never really goes all the way back down to what used to be normal for me.\n\nDoctor: During those calmer stretches you mentioned—the exceptions rather than the rule—did you ever experience periods where your mood actually dipped low? Like feeling sad, hopeless, or depressed, rather than just less energized?\n\nPatient: Well, no... not really. I mean, when I think about those calmer stretches, the ones where the buzzing finally quiets down a bit and I can actually just, you know, sit still and drink a cup of coffee without feeling like I need to immediately start drafting three different artist statements and sketching out a new sculpture all at the exact same time... during those times, I wouldn't say I feel sad. Or hopeless. It's really just... an absence of that intense, electric current. \n\nIt's actually kind of a relief, to be honest. It's like the volume on the world finally gets turned down from a screaming ten back to a manageable level. I don't feel depressed, I don't feel like crying or like everything is pointless—nothing like that. It's more just... neutral? Like I'm just existing in a normal, quiet space for a little while. I can actually focus on the mundane things, like organizing my studio space, which, let me tell you, gets completely chaotic when I'm in one of my highly energized phases. I'll have materials strewn everywhere—clay, wire, different grades of sandpaper, sketchbooks just left open on every available surface. It's just... quiet. A break from the relentless pushing and the snapping at people. Just a bit of peace before the tide comes rushing back in again. So, no, definitely not sad or hopeless. Just... resting, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past year or so, have there been any distinct periods—maybe lasting days or weeks at a time—where you felt noticeably down or depressed, where things felt heavy or hopeless, and where you had less energy or interest in your art or other activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, no, like I was just saying, I really haven't had anything like that. It's funny, because people always assume that if you're an artist, you must have these deep, dark, brooding periods where you just stare at a blank canvas and cry or something, but that's just not me. I don't get those heavy, sinking, hopeless feelings at all. I mean, if I look back over the whole year—and it's been a really wild year, honestly, with all the gallery meetings and the new series I've been working on—I can't think of a single time where I felt depressed or lost interest in my work. \n\nMy art is my entire life, you know? Even during those quiet stretches I mentioned, where I'm just organizing my studio and putting away the clay and the wire, I'm still interested in it. I'm still thinking about the next piece, even if I'm not actively, frantically building it right that second. I don't ever lose that drive or that passion. It just shifts gears. So, no, I've never had a period lasting days or weeks where I felt down or heavy or like I didn't want to create. It's always just been the high energy, the buzzing, the irritation, and then those brief, neutral resting periods where I just catch my breath. But never depression. Never anything close to that. It's almost like I don't even have the capacity for it, my brain is just wired to keep moving forward, even when it's moving a little too fast for everyone else around me.\n\nDoctor: When this elevated, energized state first started—can you remember roughly when that was? And has it been going continuously since then, or was there a clear moment when it actually began after a period of feeling more normal?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit back and try to pinpoint exactly when this whole... this whole rushing river of energy started, it's actually been quite a while. I mean, it's not like I woke up one Tuesday and suddenly everything was dialed up to an eleven, but if I trace it back, I'd say it's been about ten months. \n\nYeah, ten months of this. It was right around the time I was starting to conceptualize that large-scale mixed media installation I was telling you about—the one with the suspended wire and the terracotta fragments. I remember I was at the art supply store, and I had this sudden, absolute clarity about how the whole thing needed to come together, and I just started buying everything. And from that point on, it was just... go, go, go. The energized feeling, the irritable stretches where I just couldn't stand anyone interrupting my flow, the staying up until three or four in the morning because I had too many projects in my head and too little need for sleep... that's all been a constant companion for about ten months now. \n\nBefore that, I guess things were more normal. I mean, I was still an artist, still working, but I wasn't producing at this frantic, almost electric pace. I would actually sleep a solid eight hours, which honestly sounds a little boring to me now, but back then it was just what I did. But for the last ten months, it's just been this continuous stretch of high energy, too many ideas, and snapping at anyone who gets in my way. Even with those little brief moments of quiet I mentioned earlier, the overarching theme, the main event, has been this elevated state for almost a year now. It just hasn't really stopped since that day at the supply store.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, really, I promise I'm not holding out on you or anything. I know you're trying to be thorough, and I appreciate that, I really do. It's just... no. Never. Not even for a day or two. 

It's actually kind of fascinating if you think about it, because you'd assume that this much energy has to crash eventually, right? Like a pendulum swinging back the other way. My sister always says that to me. She's like, "You're going to burn out, you're going to hit a wall and get super depressed." But I just don't! I really don't. Even when the energy dips down into those neutral zones I was talking about, it never crosses the line into sadness or darkness. 

I've never woken up and thought, "Oh, what's the point?" or felt that heavy, dragging sensation where you can't get out of bed. Honestly, getting out of bed is the easiest thing I do all day because my brain is already ten steps ahead by the time my feet hit the floor. So, no, looking back over these last ten months, since that day at the art supply store... it's just been the electricity, the buzzing, the irritability when people are moving too slow, and then those little pockets of quiet where I just organize my wire and clay. But never sad. Never bleak. Never dark. It's just not a frequency my brain seems to tune into right now.

So when the calm hits, I just spend time putting all of that back in order. But I don't feel down about it. I don't feel that heavy, sinking feeling that people talk about when they're depressed. It's just... quiet. A break from the relentless pushing and the snapping at people. Just a bit of peace before the tide comes rushing back in again. So, no, definitely not sad or hopeless. Just... resting, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past year or so, have there been any distinct periods—maybe lasting days or weeks at a time—where you felt noticeably down or depressed, where things felt heavy or hopeless, and where you had less energy or interest in your art or other activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, no, like I was just saying, I really haven't had anything like that. It's funny, because people always assume that if you're an artist, you must have these deep, dark, brooding periods where you just stare at a blank canvas and cry or something, but that's just not me. I don't get those heavy, sinking, hopeless feelings at all. I mean, if I look back over the whole year—and it's been a really wild year, honestly, with all the gallery meetings and the new series I've been working on—I can't think of a single time where I felt depressed or lost interest in my work. \n\nMy art is my entire life, you know? Even during those quiet stretches I mentioned, where I'm just organizing my studio and putting away the clay and the wire, I'm still interested in it. I'm still thinking about the next piece, even if I'm not actively, frantically building it right that second. I don't ever lose that drive or that passion. It just shifts gears. So, no, I've never had a period lasting days or weeks where I felt down or heavy or like I didn't want to create. It's always just been the high energy, the buzzing, the irritation, and then those brief, neutral resting periods where I just catch my breath. But never depression. Never anything close to that. It's almost like I don't even have the capacity for it, my brain is just wired to keep moving forward, even when it's moving a little too fast for everyone else around me.\n\nDoctor: When this elevated, energized state first started—can you remember roughly when that was? And has it been going continuously since then, or was there a clear moment when it actually began after a period of feeling more normal?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit back and try to pinpoint exactly when this whole... this whole rushing river of energy started, it's actually been quite a while. I mean, it's not like I woke up one Tuesday and suddenly everything was dialed up to an eleven, but if I trace it back, I'd say it's been about ten months. \n\nYeah, ten months of this. It was right around the time I was starting to conceptualize that large-scale mixed media installation I was telling you about—the one with the suspended wire and the terracotta fragments. I remember I was at the art supply store, and I had this sudden, absolute clarity about how the whole thing needed to come together, and I just started buying everything. And from that point on, it was just... go, go, go. The energized feeling, the irritable stretches where I just couldn't stand anyone interrupting my flow, the staying up until three or four in the morning because I had too many projects in my head and too little need for sleep... that's all been a constant companion for about ten months now. \n\nBefore that, I guess things were more normal. I mean, I was still an artist, still working, but I wasn't producing at this frantic, almost electric pace. I would actually sleep a solid eight hours, which honestly sounds a little boring to me now, but back then it was just what I did. But for the last ten months, it's just been this continuous stretch of high energy, too many ideas, and snapping at anyone who gets in my way. Even with those little brief moments of quiet I mentioned earlier, the overarching theme, the main event, has been this elevated state for almost a year now. It just hasn't really stopped since that day at the supply store.\n\nDoctor: You've described these neutral, resting periods as feeling like a break from the intensity—just quiet and calm. But I want to make sure I'm not missing anything: during the past ten months, have you ever experienced a period where your mood actually went in the opposite direction—where you felt down, sad, or like things were bleak or dark—even if it was just for a day or two?\n\nPatient: No, really, I promise I'm not holding out on you or anything. I know you're trying to be thorough, and I appreciate that, I really do. It's just... no. Never. Not even for a day or two. \n\nIt's actually kind of fascinating if you think about it, because you'd assume that this much energy has to crash eventually, right? Like a pendulum swinging back the other way. My sister always says that to me. She's like, \"You're going to burn out, you're going to hit a wall and get super depressed.\" But I just don't! I really don't. Even when the energy dips down into those neutral zones I was talking about, it never crosses the line into sadness or darkness. \n\nI've never woken up and thought, \"Oh, what's the point?\" or felt that heavy, dragging sensation where you can't get out of bed. Honestly, getting out of bed is the easiest thing I do all day because my brain is already ten steps ahead by the time my feet hit the floor. So, no, looking back over these last ten months, since that day at the art supply store... it's just been the electricity, the buzzing, the irritability when people are moving too slow, and then those little pockets of quiet where I just organize my wire and clay. But never sad. Never bleak. Never dark. It's just not a frequency my brain seems to tune into right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, looking back before that, before the whole wire and terracotta installation took over my life... no, not really. I mean, I went to college, I got my bachelor's degree, and sure, there were stressful times. You know, finals week, or when a professor would absolutely tear apart a piece I'd spent three weeks on during a critique. That's always a blow to the ego, and I'd be upset for a day or something, maybe go eat a whole pizza with my roommates and complain about how academia stifles true creative expression. But it wasn't depression. It wasn't like a week-long thing where I couldn't function or felt hopeless.

I've just always been pretty even-keeled before this last year. I'd go to class, work in the studio, hang out with friends... it was all very standard, very normal. I never had those periods where I couldn't get out of bed or felt that heavy darkness you're talking about. Even when I was a teenager, which is when everyone is supposed to be moody and dramatic, I was mostly just focused on building my portfolio. I spent hours and hours just sketching and learning color theory. I didn't have time to be depressed, honestly. So, yeah, even going way back, before the ten months of this constant buzzing energy, I've just never really been a depressed person. It's always just been a steady baseline, right up until I got to the art supply store that day and everything just... accelerated.

They say I'm just jumping wildly from textures to budgets to these secret meanings without any bridge between them. It's incredibly frustrating because I feel like I'm being so perfectly clear, but my client notes are apparently a mess to anyone but me, my artist statements are getting flagged for being confusing, and I just can't seem to make people understand how it all fits together. It's really starting to impact my work, which is why I finally agreed to come in.\n\nDoctor: Over the same time period when you've noticed these communication difficulties, how has your mood been? Have you been feeling unusually energetic, more talkative than normal, or like you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. It's funny you ask that because it really has been a whole... a whole thing, honestly. For stretches of time, I just feel completely lit up, you know? Like this current of electricity is just buzzing right under my skin, and I feel almost unstoppable. I'll be in the studio, and every single idea I have feels so profoundly brilliant, like I'm finally tapping into the absolute truth of what art is supposed to be. I'll just go and go and go, and the energy is just so intense and expansive. \n\nBut then... it's like a coin flip. I can be riding that high, feeling so connected to everything, and then I'll be talking to a collaborator or a gallery rep, and they'll make just one tiny comment. Maybe they ask a question about the timeline, or they don't immediately grasp the concept I'm explaining, and I just... I snap. And I mean really snap. The irritation just flares up so fast and hot, and I'll lash out in a way that I know, even as it's happening, feels so much bigger and more aggressive than the situation actually warrants. It's like my patience just evaporates instantly. So it's this weird mix of feeling like a glowing genius and then suddenly being so fiercely irritated that I can barely stand to be in the same room as people who aren't on my exact wavelength.\n\nDoctor: When you think back over the past few months, have these periods of feeling energized and brilliant, followed by irritability, come in distinct episodes—like they have a clear start and finish? Or has it been more of a continuous state that's just been going on and on?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a really interesting question, because when I try to map it all out in my head, it's not like... it's not like a light switch that just flips on and off, you know? It's more like... like a tide, maybe? But a really aggressive tide. If I look back over the whole year, or at least the past several months, these stretches where I'm just bursting with energy, where I'm producing art at this incredible rate and feeling so brilliant, but also getting so incredibly irritated with everyone—those periods have definitely taken up the majority of the time. \n\nIt's actually been kind of exhausting to think about, because even though the intensity sort of rises and falls—like, some weeks I'm just buzzing at a level ten, and maybe the next week it dips to a six or a seven, where I'm still energized but maybe not snapping at people quite as violently—it's still there. The elevated state, that whole rushing current of ideas and irritation, it's been the dominant theme of my year. I'll have these brief windows where things feel a little calmer, maybe a bit more grounded, where I can actually sit and drink a cup of coffee without feeling like I need to simultaneously draft three artist statements and sketch a new sculpture. But those calmer stretches are definitely the exception, not the rule. The energized, irritable, overproductive stretches have just taken up way more of the year than the calm parts. It's like my baseline has just shifted upwards, and even when it dips, it never really goes all the way back down to what used to be normal for me.\n\nDoctor: During those calmer stretches you mentioned—the exceptions rather than the rule—did you ever experience periods where your mood actually dipped low? Like feeling sad, hopeless, or depressed, rather than just less energized?\n\nPatient: Well, no... not really. I mean, when I think about those calmer stretches, the ones where the buzzing finally quiets down a bit and I can actually just, you know, sit still and drink a cup of coffee without feeling like I need to immediately start drafting three different artist statements and sketching out a new sculpture all at the exact same time... during those times, I wouldn't say I feel sad. Or hopeless. It's really just... an absence of that intense, electric current. \n\nIt's actually kind of a relief, to be honest. It's like the volume on the world finally gets turned down from a screaming ten back to a manageable level. I don't feel depressed, I don't feel like crying or like everything is pointless—nothing like that. It's more just... neutral? Like I'm just existing in a normal, quiet space for a little while. I can actually focus on the mundane things, like organizing my studio space, which, let me tell you, gets completely chaotic when I'm in one of my highly energized phases. I'll have materials strewn everywhere—clay, wire, different grades of sandpaper, sketchbooks just left open on every available surface. So when the calm hits, I just spend time putting all of that back in order. But I don't feel down about it. I don't feel that heavy, sinking feeling that people talk about when they're depressed. It's just... quiet. A break from the relentless pushing and the snapping at people. Just a bit of peace before the tide comes rushing back in again. So, no, definitely not sad or hopeless. Just... resting, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past year or so, have there been any distinct periods—maybe lasting days or weeks at a time—where you felt noticeably down or depressed, where things felt heavy or hopeless, and where you had less energy or interest in your art or other activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, no, like I was just saying, I really haven't had anything like that. It's funny, because people always assume that if you're an artist, you must have these deep, dark, brooding periods where you just stare at a blank canvas and cry or something, but that's just not me. I don't get those heavy, sinking, hopeless feelings at all. I mean, if I look back over the whole year—and it's been a really wild year, honestly, with all the gallery meetings and the new series I've been working on—I can't think of a single time where I felt depressed or lost interest in my work. \n\nMy art is my entire life, you know? Even during those quiet stretches I mentioned, where I'm just organizing my studio and putting away the clay and the wire, I'm still interested in it. I'm still thinking about the next piece, even if I'm not actively, frantically building it right that second. I don't ever lose that drive or that passion. It just shifts gears. So, no, I've never had a period lasting days or weeks where I felt down or heavy or like I didn't want to create. It's always just been the high energy, the buzzing, the irritation, and then those brief, neutral resting periods where I just catch my breath. But never depression. Never anything close to that. It's almost like I don't even have the capacity for it, my brain is just wired to keep moving forward, even when it's moving a little too fast for everyone else around me.\n\nDoctor: When this elevated, energized state first started—can you remember roughly when that was? And has it been going continuously since then, or was there a clear moment when it actually began after a period of feeling more normal?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit back and try to pinpoint exactly when this whole... this whole rushing river of energy started, it's actually been quite a while. I mean, it's not like I woke up one Tuesday and suddenly everything was dialed up to an eleven, but if I trace it back, I'd say it's been about ten months. \n\nYeah, ten months of this. It was right around the time I was starting to conceptualize that large-scale mixed media installation I was telling you about—the one with the suspended wire and the terracotta fragments. I remember I was at the art supply store, and I had this sudden, absolute clarity about how the whole thing needed to come together, and I just started buying everything. And from that point on, it was just... go, go, go. The energized feeling, the irritable stretches where I just couldn't stand anyone interrupting my flow, the staying up until three or four in the morning because I had too many projects in my head and too little need for sleep... that's all been a constant companion for about ten months now. \n\nBefore that, I guess things were more normal. I mean, I was still an artist, still working, but I wasn't producing at this frantic, almost electric pace. I would actually sleep a solid eight hours, which honestly sounds a little boring to me now, but back then it was just what I did. But for the last ten months, it's just been this continuous stretch of high energy, too many ideas, and snapping at anyone who gets in my way. Even with those little brief moments of quiet I mentioned earlier, the overarching theme, the main event, has been this elevated state for almost a year now. It just hasn't really stopped since that day at the supply store.\n\nDoctor: You've described these neutral, resting periods as feeling like a break from the intensity—just quiet and calm. But I want to make sure I'm not missing anything: during the past ten months, have you ever experienced a period where your mood actually went in the opposite direction—where you felt down, sad, or like things were bleak or dark—even if it was just for a day or two?\n\nPatient: No, really, I promise I'm not holding out on you or anything. I know you're trying to be thorough, and I appreciate that, I really do. It's just... no. Never. Not even for a day or two. \n\nIt's actually kind of fascinating if you think about it, because you'd assume that this much energy has to crash eventually, right? Like a pendulum swinging back the other way. My sister always says that to me. She's like, \"You're going to burn out, you're going to hit a wall and get super depressed.\" But I just don't! I really don't. Even when the energy dips down into those neutral zones I was talking about, it never crosses the line into sadness or darkness. \n\nI've never woken up and thought, \"Oh, what's the point?\" or felt that heavy, dragging sensation where you can't get out of bed. Honestly, getting out of bed is the easiest thing I do all day because my brain is already ten steps ahead by the time my feet hit the floor. So, no, looking back over these last ten months, since that day at the art supply store... it's just been the electricity, the buzzing, the irritability when people are moving too slow, and then those little pockets of quiet where I just organize my wire and clay. But never sad. Never bleak. Never dark. It's just not a frequency my brain seems to tune into right now.\n\nDoctor: Before these past ten months started, going back to earlier in your life—say, in the years before the art supply store day—did you ever have periods where you felt significantly down, depressed, or low in mood that lasted for a week or more?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back before that, before the whole wire and terracotta installation took over my life... no, not really. I mean, I went to college, I got my bachelor's degree, and sure, there were stressful times. You know, finals week, or when a professor would absolutely tear apart a piece I'd spent three weeks on during a critique. That's always a blow to the ego, and I'd be upset for a day or something, maybe go eat a whole pizza with my roommates and complain about how academia stifles true creative expression. But it wasn't depression. It wasn't like a week-long thing where I couldn't function or felt hopeless.\n\nI've just always been pretty even-keeled before this last year. I'd go to class, work in the studio, hang out with friends... it was all very standard, very normal. I never had those periods where I couldn't get out of bed or felt that heavy darkness you're talking about. Even when I was a teenager, which is when everyone is supposed to be moody and dramatic, I was mostly just focused on building my portfolio. I spent hours and hours just sketching and learning color theory. I didn't have time to be depressed, honestly. So, yeah, even going way back, before the ten months of this constant buzzing energy, I've just never really been a depressed person. It's always just been a steady baseline, right up until I got to the art supply store that day and everything just... accelerated.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well, if I really cast my mind back... you know, past the terracotta, past the degree, back to when I was, say, nineteen or twenty... no. No, I really don't think so. It's funny you ask, because I've actually been trying to figure out if this has happened before, just to make sense of why it's happening now. I even called my mom last week—well, it was more like I texted her a barrage of questions at 2 AM, which she didn't appreciate—but I asked her if I was ever like this when I was younger. And she said the same thing I'm telling you: I was just a focused kid. 

I mean, sure, there were times I pulled all-nighters. But that was just normal art school stuff, you know? Like, everyone was staying up late drinking terrible dining hall coffee to finish a critique piece. It wasn't this... this electric, buzzing, "I don't need sleep because I'm fueled by the pure energy of the universe" kind of thing. It was just, "I procrastinated and now I have to paint for twelve hours straight." And I'd be exhausted afterward! I'd sleep for, like, fourteen hours the next day. 

I never had these racing thoughts back then. My thoughts were very linear, very methodical. I'd sketch, I'd plan, I'd execute. It wasn't this current situation where I have seven different projects happening simultaneously in my head and I'm irritated that my hands can't keep up with my brain. So, no, looking back at my late teens, my early twenties... I was just a regular, slightly stressed college student who slept a normal amount when I wasn't cramming. This whole high-speed, elevated, non-stop train I'm on right now? This is a completely new ride for me. It really just started that day ten months ago. Before that, it was all just steady cruising.

It's just not a frequency my brain seems to tune into right now.\n\nDoctor: Before these past ten months started, going back to earlier in your life—say, in the years before the art supply store day—did you ever have periods where you felt significantly down, depressed, or low in mood that lasted for a week or more?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back before that, before the whole wire and terracotta installation took over my life... no, not really. You know, finals week, or when a professor would absolutely tear apart a piece I'd spent three weeks on during a critique. So, yeah, even going way back, before the ten months of this constant buzzing energy, I've just never really been a depressed person. It's always just been a steady baseline, right up until I got to the art supply store that day and everything just... accelerated.\n\nDoctor: In the years before this ten-month stretch started—say, going back to your late teens or early twenties—did you ever have a period where you felt unusually energized, needed very little sleep, had racing thoughts or lots of ideas, and felt like you were operating at a higher level than normal, even if it was just for a few days or a week?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really cast my mind back... you know, past the terracotta, past the degree, back to when I was, say, nineteen or twenty... no. No, I really don't think so. It's funny you ask, because I've actually been trying to figure out if this has happened before, just to make sense of why it's happening now. I even called my mom last week—well, it was more like I texted her a barrage of questions at 2 AM, which she didn't appreciate—but I asked her if I was ever like this when I was younger. And she said the same thing I'm telling you: I was just a focused kid. \n\nI mean, sure, there were times I pulled all-nighters. But that was just normal art school stuff, you know? I'd sleep for, like, fourteen hours the next day. \n\nI never had these racing thoughts back then. My thoughts were very linear, very methodical. I'd sketch, I'd plan, I'd execute. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean... no, not really. I'm trying to think if there's any exception, because I know you're trying to be thorough and find a pattern or something. But honestly, no. I mean, sure, I've had breakups. I dated this guy, Mark, for about two years during undergrad, and when we broke up, it was... you know, it was a bummer. I think I cried for an afternoon and ate half a pint of Ben & Jerry's—Cherry Garcia, I think, which is weird because I usually hate cherry-flavored things, but it was what my roommate had in the freezer. But I wasn't *depressed*. I didn't feel empty. The next day, I was right back in the studio working on a plaster mold. 

And with my art, yeah, there are disappointments. Like I said before, a harsh critique can sting. I submitted a piece to a local gallery a few years ago—it was this really intricate mixed-media thing with reclaimed wood and textiles—and they rejected it. They sent this very polite, very generic email saying it didn't fit their current curation goals, which is just art-world speak for "we don't like it." And sure, I was annoyed. I might have vented to my sister for an hour on the phone about how subjective the art market is and how it's all about who you know. But I wasn't sad in that deep, empty way you're asking about. It didn't stop me from making art. If anything, it just made me want to make something better to prove them wrong. 

So, yeah, I've had normal human reactions to bad things happening. I'm not a robot. But that profound, heavy sadness, that emptiness where you just can't function or see the point of anything? No, never. Not even for a day. My emotional baseline has always just been very... practical, I guess? Very steady. I feel things, but they don't drag me under. Right up until ten months ago, anyway, when everything just shifted into this high gear. But even now, in this high gear, it's not sad. It's just... a lot.

It didn't stop me from making art. If anything, it just made me want to make something better to prove them wrong. \n\nSo, yeah, I've had normal human reactions to bad things happening. I'm not a robot. But that profound, heavy sadness, that emptiness where you just can't function or see the point of anything? No, never. Not even for a day. My emotional baseline has always just been very... practical, I guess? Very steady. I feel things, but they don't drag me under. Right up until ten months ago, anyway, when everything just shifted into this high gear. But even now, in this high gear, it's not sad. It's just... a lot.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, honestly, not even for a minute. It's actually been... well, the exact opposite of empty. Since that Tuesday at the art supply store—when the whole vision for the wire and terracotta just sort of downloaded into my brain—I haven't had a single day of feeling down or sad. If anything, I feel almost too full, you know? Like a vessel that's being overfilled with water and it's just constantly spilling over the edges. 

Even during those resting periods we talked about, when I'm just sitting on the floor of my studio because my fingers are literally blistered and taped up from bending wire and my body just physically demands a break, it's never a sad pause. It's never that heavy, dark feeling you're describing. I might be physically exhausted, sure, and I might just lay there staring at the ceiling fan for twelve hours, but my mind isn't empty. It's just... idling. Like a car engine that's still running but just parked for a second while I wait for my hands to stop cramping. 

I actually remember a couple of weeks ago, I had to stop working because I completely ran out of the specific 18-gauge copper wire I use. The local stores were completely out of it, and I had to order it online, which was incredibly frustrating because I absolutely hate waiting when the momentum is there. And for about three days, I couldn't work on the main structure. I think someone else might have felt down or depressed about their project being stalled, or felt a bit empty without the work, but I wasn't sad at all. I was just pacing my apartment, sketching out new angles on these giant rolls of butcher paper, completely buzzing with anticipation for the delivery truck to show up. There was no emptiness, no sadness. It's just been this relentless, constant forward motion for ten months straight. So, no, no down periods. Just this intense, non-stop drive.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Right before that Tuesday... well, if we're looking at the month or so leading up to it, my mood itself was actually pretty standard. It was a much quieter month, honestly. I was sleeping completely normally—like, getting a solid eight hours, waking up at a reasonable time, making my Earl Grey tea, watering my plants, just doing my regular morning routine. I definitely wasn't feeling unstoppable or electrified like I do now. I was just functioning at my usual pace. 

But... and this is the strange part, because I've thought about this a lot... even though my energy was totally baseline, other things were already starting to happen. It went on for several weeks before the big shift in my energy. I remember I kept hearing these... comments. Like, murmurs or whispers coming from right behind the storage shelves in my studio. I have these tall, heavy-duty metal utility racks where I keep all my bulk supplies, and I would constantly stop what I was doing, put my tools down, and walk around to the back of them because I swore I could hear people commenting on what I was doing. It sounded like they were critiquing my technique or whispering about my process, just out of sight. And there was never anyone there, obviously, because my studio is private and the door was locked. 

And on top of that, I started noticing things in my materials. Just ordinary studio supplies, you know? Like my tubs of gesso or the labels on my solvents. I started reading these hidden warnings into them. I'd look at the barcode or the standard safety instructions on a tube of paint, and it felt like the text was specifically meant for me, warning me about... well, just warning me that I needed to be careful, that something massive was coming, or that I was being watched. It was incredibly distracting. I remember spending hours just analyzing the back of a standard canvas pad, trying to decode what the manufacturer's stamp was really trying to tell me, because it felt so urgent and intentional. 

So, energy-wise, I was totally calm and sleeping fine, not revved up at all. But my mind was completely wrapped up in these hidden messages and the voices behind the shelves for a good month before the actual terracotta idea exploded into my head and the real energy kicked in.

If I look back over the whole year, or at least the past several months, these stretches where I'm just bursting with energy, where I'm producing art at this incredible rate and feeling so brilliant, but also getting so incredibly irritated with everyone—those periods have definitely taken up the majority of the time. \n\nIt's actually been kind of exhausting to think about, because even though the intensity sort of rises and falls—like, some weeks I'm just buzzing at a level ten, and maybe the next week it dips to a six or a seven, where I'm still energized but maybe not snapping at people quite as violently—it's still there. The elevated state, that whole rushing current of ideas and irritation, it's been the dominant theme of my year. It's like the volume on the world finally gets turned down from a screaming ten back to a manageable level. I can actually focus on the mundane things, like organizing my studio space, which, let me tell you, gets completely chaotic when I'm in one of my highly energized phases. I'll have materials strewn everywhere—clay, wire, different grades of sandpaper, sketchbooks just left open on every available surface. So when the calm hits, I just spend time putting all of that back in order. But I don't feel down about it. I don't feel that heavy, sinking feeling that people talk about when they're depressed. It's just... quiet. A break from the relentless pushing and the snapping at people. Just a bit of peace before the tide comes rushing back in again. So, no, definitely not sad or hopeless. It's funny, because people always assume that if you're an artist, you must have these deep, dark, brooding periods where you just stare at a blank canvas and cry or something, but that's just not me. I don't get those heavy, sinking, hopeless feelings at all. I mean, if I look back over the whole year—and it's been a really wild year, honestly, with all the gallery meetings and the new series I've been working on—I can't think of a single time where I felt depressed or lost interest in my work. \n\nMy art is my entire life, you know? So, no, I've never had a period lasting days or weeks where I felt down or heavy or like I didn't want to create. It's always just been the high energy, the buzzing, the irritation, and then those brief, neutral resting periods where I just catch my breath. But never depression. Never anything close to that. It's almost like I don't even have the capacity for it, my brain is just wired to keep moving forward, even when it's moving a little too fast for everyone else around me.\n\nDoctor: When this elevated, energized state first started—can you remember roughly when that was? I mean, it's not like I woke up one Tuesday and suddenly everything was dialed up to an eleven, but if I trace it back, I'd say it's been about ten months. \n\nYeah, ten months of this. Even with those little brief moments of quiet I mentioned earlier, the overarching theme, the main event, has been this elevated state for almost a year now. So, no, looking back over these last ten months, since that day at the art supply store... it's just been the electricity, the buzzing, the irritability when people are moving too slow, and then those little pockets of quiet where I just organize my wire and clay. But never sad. Never bleak. Never dark. It's just not a frequency my brain seems to tune into right now.\n\nDoctor: Before these past ten months started, going back to earlier in your life—say, in the years before the art supply store day—did you ever have periods where you felt significantly down, depressed, or low in mood that lasted for a week or more?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back before that, before the whole wire and terracotta installation took over my life... no, not really. I mean, I went to college, I got my bachelor's degree, and sure, there were stressful times. You know, finals week, or when a professor would absolutely tear apart a piece I'd spent three weeks on during a critique. That's always a blow to the ego, and I'd be upset for a day or something, maybe go eat a whole pizza with my roommates and complain about how academia stifles true creative expression. But it wasn't depression. It wasn't like a week-long thing where I couldn't function or felt hopeless.\n\nI've just always been pretty even-keeled before this last year. I'd go to class, work in the studio, hang out with friends... it was all very standard, very normal. I never had those periods where I couldn't get out of bed or felt that heavy darkness you're talking about. Even when I was a teenager, which is when everyone is supposed to be moody and dramatic, I was mostly just focused on building my portfolio. I spent hours and hours just sketching and learning color theory. I didn't have time to be depressed, honestly. So, yeah, even going way back, before the ten months of this constant buzzing energy, I've just never really been a depressed person. It's always just been a steady baseline, right up until I got to the art supply store that day and everything just... accelerated.\n\nDoctor: In the years before this ten-month stretch started—say, going back to your late teens or early twenties—did you ever have a period where you felt unusually energized, needed very little sleep, had racing thoughts or lots of ideas, and felt like you were operating at a higher level than normal, even if it was just for a few days or a week?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really cast my mind back... you know, past the terracotta, past the degree, back to when I was, say, nineteen or twenty... no. It's just... a lot.\n\nDoctor: During these past ten months—since that day at the art supply store until now—have there been any stretches of time, even just a few days, where your mood shifted and you felt noticeably sad, empty, or down, separate from those neutral resting periods you mentioned?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, not even for a minute. It's actually been... well, the exact opposite of empty. Since that Tuesday at the art supply store—when the whole vision for the wire and terracotta just sort of downloaded into my brain—I haven't had a single day of feeling down or sad. If anything, I feel almost too full, you know? Like a vessel that's being overfilled with water and it's just constantly spilling over the edges. \n\nEven during those resting periods we talked about, when I'm just sitting on the floor of my studio because my fingers are literally blistered and taped up from bending wire and my body just physically demands a break, it's never a sad pause. It's never that heavy, dark feeling you're describing. I might be physically exhausted, sure, and I might just lay there staring at the ceiling fan for twelve hours, but my mind isn't empty. It's just... idling. Like a car engine that's still running but just parked for a second while I wait for my hands to stop cramping. \n\nI actually remember a couple of weeks ago, I had to stop working because I completely ran out of the specific 18-gauge copper wire I use. The local stores were completely out of it, and I had to order it online, which was incredibly frustrating because I absolutely hate waiting when the momentum is there. And for about three days, I couldn't work on the main structure. I think someone else might have felt down or depressed about their project being stalled, or felt a bit empty without the work, but I wasn't sad at all. I was just pacing my apartment, sketching out new angles on these giant rolls of butcher paper, completely buzzing with anticipation for the delivery truck to show up. There was no emptiness, no sadness. It's just been this relentless, constant forward motion for ten months straight. So, no, no down periods. Just this intense, non-stop drive.\n\nDoctor: You've described this ten-month stretch as continuous high energy with brief neutral breaks. But I want to ask about the very beginning: in the days or weeks right before that Tuesday at the art supply store, how was your mood? Were you feeling normal, or had anything shifted in the weeks leading up to that moment?\n\nPatient: Right before that Tuesday... well, if we're looking at the month or so leading up to it, my mood itself was actually pretty standard. It was a much quieter month, honestly. I was sleeping completely normally—like, getting a solid eight hours, waking up at a reasonable time, making my Earl Grey tea, watering my plants, just doing my regular morning routine. I definitely wasn't feeling unstoppable or electrified like I do now. I was just functioning at my usual pace. \n\nBut... and this is the strange part, because I've thought about this a lot... even though my energy was totally baseline, other things were already starting to happen. It went on for several weeks before the big shift in my energy. I remember I kept hearing these... comments. Like, murmurs or whispers coming from right behind the storage shelves in my studio. I have these tall, heavy-duty metal utility racks where I keep all my bulk supplies, and I would constantly stop what I was doing, put my tools down, and walk around to the back of them because I swore I could hear people commenting on what I was doing. It sounded like they were critiquing my technique or whispering about my process, just out of sight. And there was never anyone there, obviously, because my studio is private and the door was locked. \n\nAnd on top of that, I started noticing things in my materials. Just ordinary studio supplies, you know? Like my tubs of gesso or the labels on my solvents. I started reading these hidden warnings into them. I'd look at the barcode or the standard safety instructions on a tube of paint, and it felt like the text was specifically meant for me, warning me about... well, just warning me that I needed to be careful, that something massive was coming, or that I was being watched. It was incredibly distracting. I remember spending hours just analyzing the back of a standard canvas pad, trying to decode what the manufacturer's stamp was really trying to tell me, because it felt so urgent and intentional. \n\nSo, energy-wise, I was totally calm and sleeping fine, not revved up at all. But my mind was completely wrapped up in these hidden messages and the voices behind the shelves for a good month before the actual terracotta idea exploded into my head and the real energy kicked in."
